    <p class="eyebrow">Part 2 · follows <a href="/blog/explainers/the-cell-that-forgot-to-stop/">The Cell That Forgot to Stop</a></p>
    <h1>The Cancer <span>Atlas</span></h1>
    <p class="dek">Ten cancers, organ by organ. Same basic story every time, but where it starts changes everything: which cells, which hits, which warning signs, and how hard it is to catch.</p>

class="map-lead" d="M138,220 L256,216"/><circle class="map-dot" cx="256" cy="216" r="7"/><text class="t b map-t" x="132" y="224" text-anchor="end">Cervix</text></a><a href="#blood" class="map-link"><path class="map-lead" d="M138,296 L228,305"/><circle class="map-dot" cx="228" cy="305" r="7"/><text class="t b map-t" x="132" y="300" text-anchor="end">Blood</text></a><a href="#lung" class="map-link"><path class="map-lead" d="M382,86 L275,106"/><circle class="map-dot" cx="275" cy="106" r="7"/><text class="t b map-t" x="388" y="90">Lung</text></a><a href="#stomach" class="map-link"><path class="map-lead" d="M382,136 L276,148"/><circle class="map-dot" cx="276" cy="148" r="7"/><text class="t b map-t" x="388" y="140">Stomach</text></a><a href="#skin" class="map-link"><path class="map-lead" d="M382,176 L320,166"/><circle class="map-dot" cx="320" cy="166" r="7"/><text class="t b map-t" x="388" y="180">Skin</text></a><a href="#colorectal" class="map-link"><path class="map-lead" d="M382,216 L260,188"/><circle class="map-dot" cx="260" cy="188" r="7"/><text class="t b map-t" x="388" y="220">Colon &amp; rectum</text></a><a href="#prostate" class="map-link"><path class="map-lead" d="M382,254 L264,232"/><circle class="map-dot" cx="264" cy="232" r="7"/><text class="t b map-t" x="388" y="258">Prostate</text></a></svg></div>
    </figure>
    <div style="display:grid;gap:14px;align-content:start">
      <p class="prose" style="font-size:17px">Part 1 showed how any cancer begins: typos pile up in one cell's accelerator, brake and repair genes until it divides without limit, invades, and spreads. Each chapter here applies that story to one organ.</p>
      <div class="cards"><a class="card" href="#breast"><b>Breast</b><span>starts in the cells lining the milk ducts and lobules</span><i>#1 in India</i></a><a class="card" href="#oral"><b>Mouth</b><span>starts in the flat cells lining the inside of the mouth</span><i>#1 in Indian men</i></a><a class="card" href="#cervical"><b>Cervix</b><span>starts in the cells of the cervix, the neck of the womb</span><i>#2 in Indian women</i></a><a class="card" href="#lung"><b>Lung</b><span>starts in the cells lining the airways and air sacs</span><i>#1 killer in India</i></a><a class="card" href="#colorectal"><b>Colorectal</b><span>starts in the lining of the large bowel (colon and rectum)</span><i>#4 in India</i></a><a class="card" href="#blood"><b>Blood</b><span>starts in the blood-making cells of the bone marrow and the immune cells of the lymph nodes</span><i>#1 in children</i></a><a class="card" href="#stomach"><b>Stomach</b><span>starts in the gland cells lining the stomach</span><i>#4 in Indian men</i></a><a class="card" href="#liver"><b>Liver</b><span>starts in the liver's main working cells, the hepatocytes</span><i>39,000 deaths</i></a><a class="card" href="#prostate"><b>Prostate</b><span>starts in the gland cells of the prostate</span><i>#5 in Indian men</i></a><a class="card" href="#skin"><b>Skin</b><span>starts in the pigment cells of the skin, the melanocytes</span><i>Rare in India</i></a></div>
    </div>
  </div>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">The one lesson every chapter repeats</span><h3 class="sec">Caught early versus after it spreads</h3></div>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">Reading a cancer's name</span><h3 class="sec">The name tells you the cell</h3></div>
  <div class="families">
    <div class="fam"><b>Carcinoma</b><p>From lining cells of skin and organs. About 8 to 9 in 10 cancers, including breast, lung, colon, mouth, cervix, prostate, stomach and liver.</p></div>
    <div class="fam"><b>Leukaemia · Lymphoma · Myeloma</b><p>From blood-forming and immune cells, in the marrow and lymph nodes.</p></div>
    <div class="fam"><b>Melanoma</b><p>From melanocytes, the pigment cells of the skin and eye.</p></div>
    <div class="fam"><b>Sarcoma</b><p>From bone, muscle, fat and other connective tissue. Rare, more common in the young.</p></div>
    <div class="fam"><b>Glioma</b><p>From the support cells of the brain and spinal cord.</p></div>
  </div>
  <p class="prose" style="font-size:16px;color:var(--ink-soft)">Two more words you will see: <strong>adenocarcinoma</strong> means a carcinoma of gland cells (most breast, colon, stomach, prostate cancers), and <strong>squamous cell carcinoma</strong> means one of flat, tile-like cells (most mouth, cervix and some lung cancers).</p>
</section>
<section class="view" id="v-breast" data-view="breast" hidden>
  <header class="c-head">
    <p class="eyebrow">Chapter 1 of 10 · starts in the cells lining the milk ducts and lobules</p>
    <h2>Breast cancer</h2>
    <p class="thesis">Cells in the milk ducts that keep growing under the push of hormones. It is the most common cancer in India, and one of the most curable when it is found early.</p>
  </header>
  <div class="stats"><div class="stat"><b>2.3 million</b><span>new cases a year worldwide</span></div><div class="stat"><b>#1 in India</b><span>30% of all cancers in women</span></div><div class="stat"><b>100%</b><span>5-year survival if found early, vs 34% after spread</span></div></div>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">1 · Where it starts</span><h3 class="sec">The cells at the root</h3></div>
  <div class="prose"><p>A breast is mostly fat. Inside it are 15 to 20 lobes of milk-making glands, the <em class="term">lobules</em>, joined to the nipple by thin tubes called <em class="term">ducts</em>.</p><p>About 8 in 10 breast cancers begin in the cells lining the ducts. Most of the rest begin in the lobules. Because a duct is a tube, an early cancer can fill it without breaking out. That stage is called DCIS (ductal carcinoma in situ), or stage 0, and it is almost always curable.</p></div>
  <figure class="plate"><div class="fig-tag"><b>Fig 1</b>Where it starts</div><div class="scroll" class="minw-600"><svg viewBox="0 0 760 340" role="img" aria-label="Side view of a breast: chest wall, fat, milk lobules and ducts leading to the nipple. A marker shows most cancers start in the duct lining. Zoomed duct cross-sections show a normal hollow duct, a duct filled with abnormal cells (DCIS, stage 0), and invasive cancer breaking through the duct wall."><defs><marker id="abr" viewBox="0 0 10 10"   <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">2 · How it starts</span><h3 class="sec">The hits that turn a cell</h3></div>
  <div class="pair">
    <div class="prose"><p>Breast cells divide in response to the hormones oestrogen and progesterone, every month from puberty to menopause. More years of hormone exposure means more divisions and more chances for typos. That is why an early first period, a late menopause, having no children or having them late, and long-term hormone therapy each raise risk a little.</p><p>Doctors sort breast cancers by what drives them. Most are <strong>hormone-receptor positive</strong>: they still respond to oestrogen, so drugs that block it work well. About 15 to 20% make too much of a growth receptor called <strong>HER2</strong>, which targeted drugs can block. <strong>Triple-negative</strong> cancers have neither switch, grow faster and are harder to treat.</p></div>
    <aside class="note"><b>The usual hits</b><ul class="hits"><li><b>BRCA1 / BRCA2</b><span>inherited faulty repair genes that raise lifetime risk to roughly 45 to 70%</span></li><li><b>PIK3CA</b><span>an accelerator, mutated in about 4 in 10 hormone-positive cancers</span></li><li><b>HER2</b><span>an accelerator gene copied many times over</span></li><li><b>TP53</b><span>the master brake, often lost in triple-negative cancer</span></li></ul></aside>
  </div>
  <div class="risk">
    <div class="fix"><h4>Risks you cannot change</h4><ul><li>Being a woman (about 1 in 100 cases are men)</li><li>Age</li><li>Family history or BRCA genes</li><li>Dense breast tissue</li><li>Early first period, late menopause</li></ul></div>
    <div class="chg"><h4>Risks you can change</h4><ul><li>Alcohol</li><li>Weight gain after menopause</li><li>Physical inactivity</li><li>Long-term combined hormone therapy</li><li>Not breastfeeding (breastfeeding lowers risk)</li></ul></div>
  </div>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">3 · How it grows</span><h3 class="sec">Stages, from earliest to most advanced</h3></div>
  <div class="stages"><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 100%, var(--eosin))"><b>Stage 0</b><span>DCIS: abnormal cells fill a duct but have not broken out</span></div><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 75%, var(--eosin))"><b>Stage I</b><span>Small tumor, up to about 2 cm, at most tiny traces in nodes</span></div><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 50%, var(--eosin))"><b>Stage II</b><span>Larger tumor, or spread to a few armpit lymph nodes</span></div><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 25%, var(--eosin))"><b>Stage III</b><span>Large, or in many nodes, the chest wall or the skin</span></div><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 0%, var(--eosin))"><b>Stage IV</b><span>Spread to bone, lungs, liver or brain</span></div></div>
  <figure class="plate"><div class="fig-tag"><b>Survival</b>5-year relative survival by how far it has spread</div>
<div class="surv"><div class="srow"><div class="lab"><b>Localized</b><span>still inside the breast</span></div><div class="track"><div class="fill" style="width:100.0%;background:var(--teal)"></div><em style="left:calc(100.0% - 10px);transform:translate(-100%,-50%);color:var(--paper)">100%</em></div></div><div class="srow"><div class="lab"><b>Regional</b><span>reached nearby lymph nodes</span></div><div class="track"><div class="fill" style="width:87.5%;background:var(--hema)"></div><em style="left:calc(87.5% - 10px);transform:translate(-100%,-50%);color:var(--paper)">87.5%</em></div></div><div class="srow"><div class="lab"><b>Distant</b><span>spread to far organs</span></div><div class="track"><div class="fill" style="width:33.8%;background:var(--eosin)"></div><em style="left:calc(33.8% - 10px);transform:translate(-100%,-50%);color:var(--paper)">33.8%</em></div></div></div>
<figcaption>Share of people alive five years after diagnosis, compared with people of the same age without cancer. US SEER data, diagnosed 2016 to 2022.  Survival in India is lower, mostly because diagnosis comes later.</figcaption></figure>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">4 · Where it travels</span><h3 class="sec">How it spreads</h3></div>
  <div class="spread">

    <div class="prose"><p>It first reaches the lymph nodes in the armpit, which is why surgeons check them. From there it favours bone, lungs, liver and brain.</p><ul><li class="p">starts: breast</li><li>armpit nodes</li><li>spine &amp; pelvis</li><li>lungs</li><li>liver</li><li>brain</li></ul></div>
  </div>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">5 · Catching it</span><h3 class="sec">Signs and screening</h3></div>
  <div class="two">
    <div class="box-l"><h4>Signs to get checked</h4><ul><li>A new lump in the breast or armpit, usually painless</li><li>A change in the size or shape of a breast</li><li>Skin dimpling, like orange peel</li><li>A nipple that turns inward, or bloody discharge</li><li>Redness, scaling or thickening of the nipple or skin</li></ul></div>
    <div class="box-l ok"><h4>Prevent it, or find it early</h4><ul><li>Know your normal: notice changes and get any new lump checked</li><li>Clinical breast examination by a health worker; India's national programme offers it from age 30</li><li>Mammogram every 1 to 2 years from 40 to 50 onwards, depending on the guideline</li><li>Women with a BRCA gene or strong family history start earlier, with MRI</li></ul></div>
  </div>
  <div class="myth"><span class="k kq">Myth</span><p class="mq">Breast cancer only happens if it runs in your family.</p><span class="k kf">Fact</span><p>About 85% of women diagnosed have no close relative with breast cancer. Age and hormones matter more than family for most people.</p></div>
  <div class="pn"><a href="#atlas">← Atlas</a><a href="#oral">Mouth cancer →</a></div>
</section>
<section class="view" id="v-oral" data-view="oral" hidden>
  <header class="c-head">
    <p class="eyebrow">Chapter 2 of 10 · starts in the flat cells lining the inside of the mouth</p>
    <h2>Mouth cancer</h2>
    <p class="thesis">India's signature cancer. Mostly caused by tobacco, chewed or smoked, and by areca nut. It often shows itself early as a white or red patch that you can see with a mirror.</p>
  </header>
  <div class="stats"><div class="stat"><b>390,000</b><span>new cases a year worldwide</span></div><div class="stat"><b>#1 in Indian men</b><span>1 in 5 cancers in men; India has about a third of world cases</span></div><div class="stat"><b>89%</b><span>5-year survival if found early, vs 36% after spread</span></div></div>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">1 · Where it starts</span><h3 class="sec">The cells at the root</h3></div>
  <div class="prose"><p>The mouth is lined with flat <em class="term">squamous cells</em> stacked in layers like tiles, constantly shed from the top and replaced from below. More than 9 in 10 mouth cancers are squamous cell carcinomas of this lining.</p><p>Where it starts depends on habit. In India it most often begins in the inner cheek and gum, exactly where a quid of gutka, khaini or paan is held for hours. In Western countries the side of the tongue is more common.</p></div>

the lining of the cheek</text></svg></div><figcaption>Left: the mouth seen from the front.   <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">2 · How it starts</span><h3 class="sec">The hits that turn a cell</h3></div>
  <div class="pair">
    <div class="prose"><p>Tobacco chemicals called nitrosamines soak straight into the lining of the cheek. Areca nut (supari) is itself a proven carcinogen, even without tobacco, and it also causes <strong>oral submucous fibrosis</strong>: the cheeks stiffen until the mouth cannot open fully. That condition can turn into cancer.</p><p>Drinking alcohol while using tobacco multiplies the risk rather than adding to it. In the throat behind the mouth, the HPV virus is a growing cause, even in people who never smoked.</p><p>Before cancer, the lining often shows warning signs: a <strong>white patch</strong> (leukoplakia) or, more dangerous, a <strong>red patch</strong> (erythroplakia).</p></div>
    <aside class="note"><b>The usual hits</b><ul class="hits"><li><b>TP53</b><span>the brake, lost in most tobacco-related cases</span></li><li><b>CDKN2A (p16)</b><span>a second brake on cell division</span></li><li><b>PIK3CA</b><span>accelerator</span></li><li><b>HPV E6 / E7</b><span>viral proteins that disable brakes in throat cancers</span></li></ul></aside>
  </div>
  <div class="risk">
    <div class="fix"><h4>Risks you cannot change</h4><ul><li>Age</li><li>Being male</li></ul></div>
    <div class="chg"><h4>Risks you can change</h4><ul><li>Chewing tobacco: gutka, khaini, mawa, paan with tobacco</li><li>Areca nut (supari), even on its own</li><li>Smoking cigarettes or bidis</li><li>Alcohol, especially with tobacco</li><li>HPV (for throat cancers)</li></ul></div>
  </div>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">3 · How it grows</span><h3 class="sec">Stages, from earliest to most advanced</h3></div>
  <div class="stages"><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 100%, var(--eosin))"><b>Pre</b><span>White or red patch, or stiff mouth (submucous fibrosis)</span></div><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 75%, var(--eosin))"><b>Stage I</b><span>Tumor 2 cm or smaller</span></div><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 50%, var(--eosin))"><b>Stage II</b><span>Between 2 and 4 cm</span></div><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 25%, var(--eosin))"><b>Stage III</b><span>Larger than 4 cm, or one small neck node</span></div><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 0%, var(--eosin))"><b>Stage IV</b><span>Into the jaw bone or skin, many nodes, or far organs</span></div></div>
  <figure class="plate"><div class="fig-tag"><b>Survival</b>5-year relative survival by how far it has spread</div>
<div class="surv"><div class="srow"><div class="lab"><b>Localized</b><span>still in the mouth</span></div><div class="track"><div class="fill" style="width:88.7%;background:var(--teal)"></div><em style="left:calc(88.7% - 10px);transform:translate(-100%,-50%);color:var(--paper)">88.7%</em></div></div><div class="srow"><div class="lab"><b>Regional</b><span>reached neck nodes</span></div><div class="track"><div class="fill" style="width:69.7%;background:var(--hema)"></div><em style="left:calc(69.7% - 10px);transform:translate(-100%,-50%);color:var(--paper)">69.7%</em></div></div><div class="srow"><div class="lab"><b>Distant</b><span>spread to far organs</span></div><div class="track"><div class="fill" style="width:36.0%;background:var(--eosin)"></div><em style="left:calc(36.0% - 10px);transform:translate(-100%,-50%);color:var(--paper)">36%</em></div></div></div>
<figcaption>Share of people alive five years after diagnosis, compared with people of the same age without cancer. US SEER data, diagnosed 2016 to 2022. US figures for mouth and throat combined. Survival in India is lower, mostly because diagnosis comes later.</figcaption></figure>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">4 · Where it travels</span><h3 class="sec">How it spreads</h3></div>
  <div class="spread">
    <svg viewBox="10 0 180 420" role="img" aria-label="Body map: starts in the mouth, and most often spreads to neck nodes, lungs."><defs><marker id="aoral" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="8" refY="5" markerWidth="7" markerHeight="7" orient="auto-start-reverse"><path class="mk" d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z"/></marker><marker id="boral" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="8" refY="5" markerWidth="7" markerHeight="7" orient="auto-start-reverse"><path class="mk bad" d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z"/></marker></defs><path class="bm-body" d="M88,60 L112,60 L114,72 C140,74 156,82 160,98 L172,190 C174,200 170,206 164,204 L150,128 L146,200 L150,250 L144,402 C143,410 128,410 127,402 L108,262 L92,262 L73,402 C72,410 57,410 56,402 L50,250 L54,200 L50,128 L36,204 C30,206 26,200 28,190 L40,98 C44,82 60,74 86,72 Z"/><circle class="bm-body" cx="100" cy="36" r="24"/><ellipse class="bm-organ" cx="85" cy="112" rx="13" ry="25"/><ellipse class="bm-organ" cx="115" cy="112" rx="13" ry="25"/><path class="bm-organ" d="M70,142 C80,136 104,136 108,142 C108,152 96,160 76,158 C70,154 68,148 70,142 Z"/><ellipse class="bm-organ" cx="116" cy="148" rx="11" ry="8" transform="rotate(-20 116 148)"/><rect class="bm-organ line-only" x="78" y="168" width="44" height="34" rx="12"/><ellipse class="bm-organ" cx="100" cy="218" rx="9" ry="7"/><line class="bm-bone" x1="70" y1="262" x2="66" y2="360"/><line class="bm-bone" x1="130" y1="262" x2="134" y2="360"/><line class="bm-bone" x1="100" y1="76" x2="100" y2="250"/><path class="spread-line" d="M100,50 Q118.0,59.0 100,68" marker-end="url(#boral)"/><path class="spread-line" d="M100,50 Q89.5,79.0 115,108" marker-end="url(#boral)"/><circle class="spread-dot" cx="100" cy="68" r="5"/><circle class="spread-dot" cx="115" cy="108" r="5"/><circle class="primary-dot pulse" cx="100" cy="50" r="8"/></svg>
    <div class="prose"><p>It spreads mainly through the lymph channels to the nodes of the neck, which is why surgeons often remove them. Distant spread, usually to the lungs, comes late.</p><ul><li class="p">starts: mouth</li><li>neck nodes</li><li>lungs</li></ul></div>
  </div>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">5 · Catching it</span><h3 class="sec">Signs and screening</h3></div>
  <div class="two">
    <div class="box-l"><h4>Signs to get checked</h4><ul><li>An ulcer or sore that does not heal in 2 weeks</li><li>A white or red patch inside the mouth</li><li>A lump or thickening in the cheek</li><li>Difficulty opening the mouth</li><li>Pain while chewing or swallowing, or loose teeth</li><li>A lump in the neck</li></ul></div>
    <div class="box-l ok"><h4>Prevent it, or find it early</h4><ul><li>Look in the mirror once a month, cheeks and tongue included</li><li>Visual mouth check by a doctor or dentist; India's national programme screens from 30</li><li>A large trial in Kerala found regular mouth checks lowered deaths among tobacco users</li><li>Quitting tobacco and areca nut lowers risk year by year</li></ul></div>
  </div>
  <div class="myth"><span class="k kq">Myth</span><p class="mq">Chewing tobacco is safer than smoking.</p><span class="k kf">Fact</span><p>Chewed tobacco is the leading cause of mouth cancer in India. "Safe" pan masala with only areca nut also causes cancer.</p></div>
  <div class="pn"><a href="#breast">← Breast cancer</a><a href="#cervical">Cervical cancer →</a></div>
</section>
<section class="view" id="v-cervical" data-view="cervical" hidden>
  <header class="c-head">
    <p class="eyebrow">Chapter 3 of 10 · starts in the cells of the cervix, the neck of the womb</p>
    <h2>Cervical cancer</h2>
    <p class="thesis">Almost every case is caused by a common virus, HPV. It is the cancer we best know how to eliminate: a vaccine stops the infection and a simple test finds changes years before they become cancer.</p>
  </header>
  <div class="stats"><div class="stat"><b>661,000</b><span>new cases and 348,000 deaths a year worldwide</span></div><div class="stat"><b>#2 in Indian women</b><span>about 1 in 10 cancers in women</span></div><div class="stat"><b>92%</b><span>5-year survival if found early, vs 21% after spread</span></div></div>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">1 · Where it starts</span><h3 class="sec">The cells at the root</h3></div>
  <div class="prose"><p>The cervix is the narrow neck of the uterus that opens into the vagina. Its outer surface is covered by flat squamous cells; the canal inside is lined with column-shaped gland cells.</p><p>Where the two meet is the <em class="term">transformation zone</em>, a place of constant cell change. Nearly all cervical cancers begin there.</p></div>
  <figure class="plate"><div class="fig-tag"><b>Fig 1</b>Where it starts</div><div class="scroll" class="minw-600"><svg viewBox="0 0 760 344" role="img" aria-label="Front view of the uterus, ovaries, cervix and vagina, with a marker at the transformation zone of the cervix. To the right, an HPV particle and four zoomed views of the cervical lining: CIN 1 with abnormal cells in the bottom third, CIN 2 in two thirds, CIN 3 in the full thickness, and cancer breaking through. This usually takes 15 to 20 years."><defs><marker id="acv" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="8" refY="5" markerWidth="7" markerHeight="7" orient="auto-start-reverse"><path class="mk" d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z"/></marker><marker id="bcv" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="8" refY="5" markerWidth="7" markerHeight="7" orient="auto-start-reverse"><path class="mk bad" d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z"/></marker></defs><path class="tube" d="M128,70 C96,64 70,66 46,86"/><path class="tube" d="M252,70 C284,64 310,66 334,86"/><ellipse class="ovary" cx="52" cy="112" rx="20" ry="13"/><ellipse class="ovary" cx="328" cy="112" rx="20" ry="13"/><path class="uterus" d="M120,64 C120,40 260,40 260,64 C262,124 240,172 214,196 L214,254 L166,254 L166,196 C140,172 118,124 120,64 Z"/><path class="uterus-in" d="M146,72 L234,72 L196,160 L184,160 Z"/><line class="canal" x1="190" y1="162" x2="190" y2="254"/><path class="vagina" d="M164,254 L216,254 L222,326 L158,326 Z"/><circle class="origin" cx="190" cy="252" r="12"/><circle class="origin-dot" cx="190" cy="252" r="3"/><text class="s" x="190" y="32" text-anchor="middle">uterus (womb)</text><text class="s" x="52" y="140" text-anchor="middle">ovary</text><text class="t b" x="232" y="222">cervix</text><text class="s" x="232" y="238">neck of the womb</text><text class="s" x="230" y="300">vagina</text><line class="line bad" x1="177" y1="256" x2="146" y2="262"/><text class="t b red" x="14" y="262">transformation zone:</text><text class="s" x="14" y="278">where flat and gland</text><text class="s" x="14" y="293">cells meet. CIN 1 often goes away by itself; CIN 2 and 3 are treated in a short clinic procedure. All three are precancers, not cancer.</figcaption></figure>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">2 · How it starts</span><h3 class="sec">The hits that turn a cell</h3></div>
  <div class="pair">
    <div class="prose"><p>HPV (human papillomavirus) spreads through skin-to-skin sexual contact, and most people catch it at some point. Most infections clear within one to two years. In a minority, a high-risk type, especially HPV 16 or 18, lingers.</p><p>The virus then makes two proteins, <strong>E6</strong> and <strong>E7</strong>, that grab and disable the cell's two most important brakes, <em>p53</em> and <em>RB</em>. With the brakes gone, abnormal cells slowly fill the lining from the bottom up, through precancer stages called <strong>CIN 1, 2 and 3</strong>, before breaking through. That slow climb is what screening is designed to catch.</p><p>Smoking, HIV and other causes of weak immunity, and many full-term pregnancies make it harder to clear the virus.</p></div>
    <aside class="note"><b>The usual hits</b><ul class="hits"><li><b>HPV E6</b><span>destroys p53, the master brake</span></li><li><b>HPV E7</b><span>disables RB, the cell-cycle brake</span></li><li><b>PIK3CA</b><span>accelerator, a common later hit</span></li></ul></aside>
  </div>
  <div class="risk">
    <div class="fix"><h4>Risks you cannot change</h4><ul><li>Age (peaks in the 40s and 50s)</li><li>Weak immunity, including HIV</li></ul></div>
    <div class="chg"><h4>Risks you can change</h4><ul><li>Not being vaccinated against HPV</li><li>Never being screened</li><li>Smoking</li><li>Many sexual partners, or a partner with many</li></ul></div>
  </div>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">3 · How it grows</span><h3 class="sec">Stages, from earliest to most advanced</h3></div>
  <div class="stages"><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 100%, var(--eosin))"><b>CIN</b><span>Precancer in the lining, treatable in a clinic visit</span></div><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 75%, var(--eosin))"><b>Stage I</b><span>Cancer only in the cervix</span></div><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 50%, var(--eosin))"><b>Stage II</b><span>Beyond the cervix, into the upper vagina</span></div><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 25%, var(--eosin))"><b>Stage III</b><span>Lower vagina, pelvic wall or blocking a kidney</span></div><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 0%, var(--eosin))"><b>Stage IV</b><span>Bladder, rectum or far organs</span></div></div>
  <figure class="plate"><div class="fig-tag"><b>Survival</b>5-year relative survival by how far it has spread</div>
<div class="surv"><div class="srow"><div class="lab"><b>Localized</b><span>only in the cervix</span></div><div class="track"><div class="fill" style="width:91.8%;background:var(--teal)"></div><em style="left:calc(91.8% - 10px);transform:translate(-100%,-50%);color:var(--paper)">91.8%</em></div></div><div class="srow"><div class="lab"><b>Regional</b><span>beyond the cervix, nearby nodes</span></div><div class="track"><div class="fill" style="width:64.0%;background:var(--hema)"></div><em style="left:calc(64.0% - 10px);transform:translate(-100%,-50%);color:var(--paper)">64%</em></div></div><div class="srow"><div class="lab"><b>Distant</b><span>spread to far organs</span></div><div class="track"><div class="fill" style="width:20.5%;background:var(--eosin)"></div><em style="left:calc(20.5% + 8px);color:var(--ink)">20.5%</em></div></div></div>
<figcaption>Share of people alive five years after diagnosis, compared with people of the same age without cancer. US SEER data, diagnosed 2016 to 2022.  Survival in India is lower, mostly because diagnosis comes later.</figcaption></figure>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">4 · Where it travels</span><h3 class="sec">How it spreads</h3></div>
  <div class="spread">
    <svg viewBox="10 0 180 420" role="img" aria-label="Body map: starts in the cervix, and most often spreads to pelvic nodes, lungs, liver, spine &amp; pelvis."><defs><marker id="acervical"     <div class="prose"><p>It first grows into nearby tissue and pelvic lymph nodes, then can reach the lungs, liver and bones.</p><ul><li class="p">starts: cervix</li><li>pelvic nodes</li><li>lungs</li><li>liver</li><li>spine &amp; pelvis</li></ul></div>
  </div>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">5 · Catching it</span><h3 class="sec">Signs and screening</h3></div>
  <div class="two">
    <div class="box-l"><h4>Signs to get checked</h4><ul><li>Bleeding between periods, after sex, or after menopause</li><li>Unusual or bad-smelling discharge</li><li>Pelvic pain or pain during sex</li><li>Early stages usually cause no symptoms, which is why screening matters</li></ul></div>
    <div class="box-l ok"><h4>Prevent it, or find it early</h4><ul><li>HPV vaccine at age 9 to 14; WHO says one dose is enough</li><li>HPV test every 5 to 10 years from age 30, or a Pap test</li><li>VIA, the vinegar test: abnormal areas turn white, so precancers can be found and treated in the same visit</li><li>WHO's goal: 90% vaccinated, 70% screened, 90% treated by 2030</li></ul></div>
  </div>
  <div class="myth"><span class="k kq">Myth</span><p class="mq">The HPV vaccine is only for girls who are sexually active.</p><span class="k kf">Fact</span><p>It works best at 9 to 14, before any exposure to the virus. Boys can be vaccinated too.</p></div>
  <div class="pn"><a href="#oral">← Mouth cancer</a><a href="#lung">Lung cancer →</a></div>
</section>
<section class="view" id="v-lung" data-view="lung" hidden>
  <header class="c-head">
    <p class="eyebrow">Chapter 4 of 10 · starts in the cells lining the airways and air sacs</p>
    <h2>Lung cancer</h2>
    <p class="thesis">The world's most common and deadliest cancer. Most cases come from tobacco smoke, yet a growing share strikes people who never smoked.</p>
  </header>
  <div class="stats"><div class="stat"><b>2.5 million</b><span>new cases a year, the most common worldwide</span></div><div class="stat"><b>#1 killer in India</b><span>the top cause of cancer death</span></div><div class="stat"><b>66%</b><span>5-year survival if found early, vs 11% after spread</span></div></div>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">1 · Where it starts</span><h3 class="sec">The cells at the root</h3></div>
  <div class="prose"><p>Air travels down the windpipe, splits into two bronchi, then branches again and again into ever-smaller tubes that end in hundreds of millions of tiny air sacs called <em class="term">alveoli</em>.</p><p>Lung cancers are named by the cell they start in. <strong>Non-small cell</strong> lung cancer (about 85%) includes <strong>adenocarcinoma</strong>, which usually starts in the small outer airways and is the most common type overall, and <strong>squamous cell</strong> cancer, which starts in the big central airways. <strong>Small cell</strong> lung cancer (about 15%) grows fast, spreads early and is almost only seen in smokers.</p></div>

r="3.6"/><circle class="origin" cx="341" cy="132" r="10"/><circle class="origin-dot" cx="341" cy="132" r="3"/><circle class="origin" cx="535" cy="254" r="10"/><circle class="origin-dot" cx="535" cy="254" r="3"/><text class="s" x="400" y="24">windpipe (trachea)</text><line class="line bad" x1="330" y1="132" x2="186" y2="104"/><text class="t b red" x="16" y="82">Central: big airways</text><text class="s" x="16" y="99">squamous cell &amp; small cell</text><text class="s" x="16" y="114">almost always in smokers</text><line class="line bad" x1="546" y1="254" x2="590" y2="254"/><text class="t b red" x="596" y="236">Outer: small airways</text><text class="s" x="596" y="252">adenocarcinoma, the most</text><text class="s" x="596" y="267">common type, and the usual</text><text class="s" x="596" y="282">one in never-smokers</text><text class="s" x="596" y="120">air sacs (alveoli)</text><line class="line soft" x1="592" y1="117" x2="552" y2="150"/><text class="s" x="16" y="300">bronchi branch about 23 times</text><text class="s" x="16" y="315">into hundreds of millions of air sacs</text></svg></div><figcaption>Where in the lung a cancer starts depends on the cell type, and that predicts who gets it. Central tumors tend to cause cough and bleeding early; outer tumors can grow silently.</figcaption></figure>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">2 · How it starts</span><h3 class="sec">The hits that turn a cell</h3></div>
  <div class="pair">
    <div class="prose"><p>Every puff delivers a mix of more than 70 cancer-causing chemicals straight onto the airway lining. Researchers estimate that a pack-a-day smoker builds up about 150 extra mutations in every lung cell each year.</p><p>In people who never smoked, the culprits are second-hand smoke, outdoor air pollution, smoke from cooking on wood or coal, radon gas, and specific mutations such as <em>EGFR</em> and <em>ALK</em>. EGFR mutations are especially common in Asian non-smokers, and they matter because pills that block them can shrink these tumors dramatically.</p></div>
    <aside class="note"><b>The usual hits</b><ul class="hits"><li><b>KRAS</b><span>accelerator, common in smokers' adenocarcinoma</span></li><li><b>EGFR</b><span>accelerator, common in never-smokers in Asia; blocked by targeted pills</span></li><li><b>ALK fusion</b><span>two genes fused into one accelerator; also targetable</span></li><li><b>TP53</b><span>the brake, lost in most smokers' tumors</span></li></ul></aside>
  </div>
  <div class="risk">
    <div class="fix"><h4>Risks you cannot change</h4><ul><li>Age</li><li>Family history</li><li>Earlier lung disease such as TB scarring or COPD</li></ul></div>
    <div class="chg"><h4>Risks you can change</h4><ul><li>Smoking cigarettes or bidis</li><li>Second-hand smoke</li><li>Air pollution and indoor cooking smoke</li><li>Radon, asbestos and silica dust at work</li></ul></div>
  </div>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">3 · How it grows</span><h3 class="sec">Stages, from earliest to most advanced</h3></div>
  <div class="stages"><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 100%, var(--eosin))"><b>Stage I</b><span>Small tumor only in the lung</span></div><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 67%, var(--eosin))"><b>Stage II</b><span>Larger, or in lymph nodes inside the lung</span></div><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 33%, var(--eosin))"><b>Stage III</b><span>Spread to nodes in the middle of the chest</span></div><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 0%, var(--eosin))"><b>Stage IV</b><span>In both lungs, fluid around the lung, or far organs</span></div></div>
  <figure class="plate"><div class="fig-tag"><b>Survival</b>5-year relative survival by how far it has spread</div>
<div class="surv"><div class="srow"><div class="lab"><b>Localized</b><span>only in the lung</span></div><div class="track"><div class="fill" style="width:65.5%;background:var(--teal)"></div><em style="left:calc(65.5% - 10px);transform:translate(-100%,-50%);color:var(--paper)">65.5%</em></div></div><div class="srow"><div class="lab"><b>Regional</b><span>reached nearby nodes</span></div><div class="track"><div class="fill" style="width:38.2%;background:var(--hema)"></div><em style="left:calc(38.2% - 10px);transform:translate(-100%,-50%);color:var(--paper)">38.2%</em></div></div><div class="srow"><div class="lab"><b>Distant</b><span>spread to far organs</span></div><div class="track"><div class="fill" style="width:10.5%;background:var(--eosin)"></div><em style="left:calc(10.5% + 8px);color:var(--ink)">10.5%</em></div></div></div>
<figcaption>Share of people alive five years after diagnosis, compared with people of the same age without cancer. US SEER data, diagnosed 2016 to 2022.  Survival in India is lower, mostly because diagnosis comes later.</figcaption></figure>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">4 · Where it travels</span><h3 class="sec">How it spreads</h3></div>
  <div class="spread">
    <svg viewBox="10 0 180 420" role="img" aria-label="Body map: starts in the lungs, and most often spreads to brain, spine &amp; pelvis, liver, adrenal glands."><defs><marker id="alung" viewBox="0 0 class="bm-organ line-only" x="78" y="168" width="44" height="34" rx="12"/><ellipse class="bm-organ" cx="100" cy="218" rx="9" ry="7"/><line class="bm-bone" x1="70" y1="262" x2="66" y2="360"/><line class="bm-bone" x1="130" y1="262" x2="134" y2="360"/><line class="bm-bone" x1="100" y1="76" x2="100" y2="250"/><path class="spread-line" d="M115,108 Q125.5,69.0 100,30" marker-end="url(#blung)"/><path class="spread-line" d="M115,108 Q125.5,172.0 100,236" marker-end="url(#blung)"/><path class="spread-line" d="M115,108 Q118.5,128.0 86,148" marker-end="url(#blung)"/><path class="spread-line" d="M115,108 Q129.5,136.0 108,164" marker-end="url(#blung)"/><circle class="spread-dot" cx="100" cy="30" r="5"/><circle class="spread-dot" cx="100" cy="236" r="5"/><circle class="spread-dot" cx="86" cy="148" r="5"/><circle class="spread-dot" cx="108" cy="164" r="5"/><circle class="primary-dot pulse" cx="115" cy="108" r="8"/></svg>
    <div class="prose"><p>Lung cancer reaches the blood easily, so it often spreads before it is found: to the brain, bones, liver and adrenal glands.</p><ul><li class="p">starts: lungs</li><li>brain</li><li>spine &amp; pelvis</li><li>liver</li><li>adrenal glands</li></ul></div>
  </div>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">5 · Catching it</span><h3 class="sec">Signs and screening</h3></div>
  <div class="two">
    <div class="box-l"><h4>Signs to get checked</h4><ul><li>A cough that does not go away, or changes</li><li>Coughing up blood</li><li>Breathlessness or chest pain</li><li>Hoarse voice</li><li>Repeated chest infections</li><li>Weight loss and tiredness</li></ul></div>
    <div class="box-l ok"><h4>Prevent it, or find it early</h4><ul><li>Low-dose CT scan every year for heavy smokers aged 50 to 80 (US guideline)</li><li>Quitting at any age helps; lung cancer risk falls by about half within 10 to 15 years</li><li>In India, a persistent cough is often treated as TB first, so ask about cancer if treatment does not help</li><li>Cook with clean fuel and ventilate kitchens</li></ul></div>
  </div>
  <div class="myth"><span class="k kq">Myth</span><p class="mq">Only smokers get lung cancer.</p><span class="k kf">Fact</span><p>Between 10 and 25% of lung cancers occur in people who never smoked, and the share is higher among women in Asia.</p></div>
  <div class="pn"><a href="#cervical">← Cervical cancer</a><a href="#colorectal">Colorectal cancer →</a></div>
</section>
<section class="view" id="v-colorectal" data-view="colorectal" hidden>
  <header class="c-head">
    <p class="eyebrow">Chapter 5 of 10 · starts in the lining of the large bowel (colon and rectum)</p>
    <h2>Colorectal cancer</h2>
    <p class="thesis">It usually grows from a harmless-looking polyp over about ten years. That slow start makes it one of the most preventable cancers: remove the polyp and you remove the cancer.</p>
  </header>
  <div class="stats"><div class="stat"><b>1.9 million</b><span>new cases a year, #3 worldwide</span></div><div class="stat"><b>#4 in India</b><span>and rising in younger adults</span></div><div class="stat"><b>91%</b><span>5-year survival if found early, vs 17% after spread</span></div></div>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">1 · Where it starts</span><h3 class="sec">The cells at the root</h3></div>
  <div class="prose"><p>The colon and rectum are the last 1.5 metres of the gut. Their lining is folded into millions of tiny pits called crypts, and cells at the bottom of each pit divide nonstop to replace the whole lining every few days.</p><p>That constant division makes this lining a common starting point. Almost all colorectal cancers are adenocarcinomas of the lining, and most pass through a visible stage first: a <em class="term">polyp</em>, a small mushroom of extra cells.</p></div>
  <figure class="plate"><div class="fig-tag"><b>Fig 1</b>Where it starts</div><div class="scroll" class="minw-600"><svg viewBox="0 0 760 344" role="img" aria-label="Cross-section of the colon wall in four stages: normal lining, a small polyp, a large polyp, and cancer invading through the muscle layer toward a blood vessel. A note shows polyps can be removed during colonoscopy. The whole path usually takes about 10 years."><defs><marker id="aco" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="8" refY="5" markerWidth="7" markerHeight="7" orient="auto-start-reverse"><path class="mk" d="M0,0 L10,5 cells</text><text class="s" x="8" y="175">submucosa</text><text class="s" x="8" y="212">muscle</text><text class="s" x="95" y="26" text-anchor="middle">lining renews every few days</text><line cancer</text></svg></div><figcaption>The adenoma-carcinoma sequence. Each step needs a new hit, and the whole path usually takes about ten years. A polyp removed at step two or three never becomes cancer.</figcaption></figure>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">2 · How it starts</span><h3 class="sec">The hits that turn a cell</h3></div>
  <div class="pair">
    <div class="prose"><p>This is the textbook example of cancer step by step, the path drawn in Part 1. Losing the <em>APC</em> brake makes a small polyp. A stuck <em>KRAS</em> accelerator makes it grow. Losing <em>TP53</em> lets it invade.</p><p>Diet matters here more than for most cancers. Processed meat is a proven cause and red meat a probable one, while fibre is protective. Obesity, alcohol, smoking and inactivity add risk. About 1 in 30 cases comes from <strong>Lynch syndrome</strong>, an inherited fault in DNA repair genes.</p></div>
    <aside class="note"><b>The usual hits</b><ul class="hits"><li><b>APC</b><span>the gatekeeper brake, lost first in most cases</span></li><li><b>KRAS</b><span>accelerator stuck on, as polyps grow</span></li><li><b>TP53</b><span>brake lost when the polyp turns to cancer</span></li><li><b>MLH1, MSH2</b><span>repair genes broken in Lynch syndrome</span></li></ul></aside>
  </div>
  <div class="risk">
    <div class="fix"><h4>Risks you cannot change</h4><ul><li>Age, though cases under 50 are rising</li><li>Family history or Lynch syndrome</li><li>Inflammatory bowel disease (ulcerative colitis, Crohn's)</li><li>Earlier polyps</li></ul></div>
    <div class="chg"><h4>Risks you can change</h4><ul><li>Processed and red meat</li><li>Low fibre diet</li><li>Obesity and inactivity</li><li>Alcohol and smoking</li></ul></div>
  </div>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">3 · How it grows</span><h3 class="sec">Stages, from earliest to most advanced</h3></div>
  <div class="stages"><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 100%, var(--eosin))"><b>Stage 0</b><span>Abnormal cells only in the innermost lining</span></div><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 75%, var(--eosin))"><b>Stage I</b><span>Into the deeper layers or muscle of the wall</span></div><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 50%, var(--eosin))"><b>Stage II</b><span>Through the bowel wall</span></div><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 25%, var(--eosin))"><b>Stage III</b><span>Nearby lymph nodes</span></div><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 0%, var(--eosin))"><b>Stage IV</b><span>Liver, lungs or the lining of the belly</span></div></div>
  <figure class="plate"><div class="fig-tag"><b>Survival</b>5-year relative survival by how far it has spread</div>
<div class="surv"><div class="srow"><div class="lab"><b>Localized</b><span>still in the bowel wall</span></div><div class="track"><div class="fill" style="width:91.3%;background:var(--teal)"></div><em style="left:calc(91.3% - 10px);transform:translate(-100%,-50%);color:var(--paper)">91.3%</em></div></div><div class="srow"><div class="lab"><b>Regional</b><span>reached nearby nodes</span></div><div class="track"><div class="fill" style="width:75.2%;background:var(--hema)"></div><em style="left:calc(75.2% - 10px);transform:translate(-100%,-50%);color:var(--paper)">75.2%</em></div></div><div class="srow"><div class="lab"><b>Distant</b><span>spread to far organs</span></div><div class="track"><div class="fill" style="width:16.9%;background:var(--eosin)"></div><em style="left:calc(16.9% + 8px);color:var(--ink)">16.9%</em></div></div></div>
<figcaption>Share of people alive five years after diagnosis, compared with people of the same age without cancer. US SEER data, diagnosed 2016 to 2022.  Survival in India is lower, mostly because diagnosis comes later.</figcaption></figure>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">4 · Where it travels</span><h3 class="sec">How it spreads</h3></div>
  <div class="spread">
    <svg viewBox="10 0 180 420" role="img" aria-label="Body map: starts in the colon, and most often spreads to liver, lungs, belly lining."><defs><marker id="acolorectal" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="8" refY="5" markerWidth="7" markerHeight="7" orient="auto-start-reverse"><path class="mk" d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z"/></marker><marker id="bcolorectal" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="8" refY="5" markerWidth="7" markerHeight="7" orient="auto-start-reverse"><path class="mk bad" d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z"/></marker></defs><path class="bm-body" d="M88,60 L112,60 L114,72 C140,74 156,82 160,98 L172,190 C174,200 170,206 164,204 L150,128 L146,200 L150,250 L144,402 C143,410 128,410 127,402 L108,262 L92,262 L73,402 C72,410 57,410 56,402 L50,250 L54,200 L50,128 L36,204 C30,206 26,200 28,190 L40,98 C44,82 60,74 86,72 Z"/><circle class="bm-body" cx="100" cy="36" r="24"/><ellipse class="bm-organ" cx="85" cy="112" rx="13" ry="25"/><ellipse class="bm-organ" cx="115" cy="112" rx="13" ry="25"/><path class="bm-organ" d="M70,142 C80,136 104,136 108,142 C108,152 96,160 76,158 C70,154 68,148 70,142 Z"/><ellipse class="bm-organ" cx="116" cy="148" rx="11" ry="8" transform="rotate(-20 116 148)"/><rect class="bm-organ line-only" x="78" y="168" width="44" height="34" rx="12"/><ellipse class="bm-organ" cx="100" cy="218" rx="9" ry="7"/><line class="bm-bone" x1="70" y1="262" x2="66" y2="360"/><line class="bm-bone" x1="130" y1="262" x2="134" y2="360"/><line class="bm-bone" x1="100" y1="76" x2="100" y2="250"/><path class="spread-line" d="M100,186 Q111.0,167.0 86,148" marker-end="url(#bcolorectal)"/><path class="spread-line" d="M100,186 Q89.5,147.0 115,108" marker-end="url(#bcolorectal)"/><path class="spread-line" d="M100,186 Q87.0,192.0 110,198" marker-end="url(#bcolorectal)"/><circle class="spread-dot" cx="86" cy="148" r="5"/><circle class="spread-dot" cx="115" cy="108" r="5"/><circle class="spread-dot" cx="110" cy="198" r="5"/><circle class="primary-dot pulse" cx="100" cy="186" r="8"/></svg>
    <div class="prose"><p>Blood from the gut drains straight to the liver, so the liver is almost always the first organ it reaches. Next come the lungs and the lining of the belly.</p><ul><li class="p">starts: colon</li><li>liver</li><li>lungs</li><li>belly lining</li></ul></div>
  </div>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">5 · Catching it</span><h3 class="sec">Signs and screening</h3></div>
  <div class="two">
    <div class="box-l"><h4>Signs to get checked</h4><ul><li>Blood in the stool, or bleeding from the rectum</li><li>A change in bowel habit lasting weeks</li><li>Belly pain or cramps</li><li>Feeling the bowel has not emptied</li><li>Unexplained anaemia or tiredness</li><li>Weight loss</li></ul></div>
    <div class="box-l ok"><h4>Prevent it, or find it early</h4><ul><li>Colonoscopy every 10 years from age 45 (US); polyps are removed during the test</li><li>Or a yearly stool test (FIT) that looks for hidden blood</li><li>Earlier and more often with family history or Lynch syndrome</li><li>Eat fibre, limit processed meat, stay active</li></ul></div>
  </div>
  <div class="myth"><span class="k kq">Myth</span><p class="mq">Rectal bleeding is just piles.</p><span class="k kf">Fact</span><p>Often it is, but bleeding is also the most common sign of bowel cancer. Get it checked, especially after 40 or with a change in bowel habit.</p></div>
  <div class="pn"><a href="#lung">← Lung cancer</a><a href="#blood">Blood cancers →</a></div>
</section>
<section class="view" id="v-blood" data-view="blood" hidden>
  <header class="c-head">
    <p class="eyebrow">Chapter 6 of 10 · starts in the blood-making cells of the bone marrow and the immune cells of the lymph nodes</p>
    <h2>Blood cancers</h2><p class="sub">Leukaemia · Lymphoma · Myeloma</p>
    <p class="thesis">There is no lump to find. These cancers start in the factory that makes your blood, so they are spread through the body from day one, and they are treated with drugs, radiation and stem cell transplants rather than surgery.</p>
  </header>
  <div class="stats"><div class="stat"><b>1.2 million+</b><span>new cases a year worldwide, all three types</span></div><div class="stat"><b>#1 in children</b><span>leukaemia is the most common childhood cancer</span></div><div class="stat"><b>64 to 74%</b><span>overall 5-year survival, depending on type</span></div></div>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">1 · Where it starts</span><h3 class="sec">The cells at the root</h3></div>
  <div class="prose"><p>Every blood cell descends from <em class="term">stem cells</em> in the bone marrow, the soft tissue inside your bones. Together they make hundreds of billions of new blood cells every day.</p><p>The family tree splits in two. The <strong>myeloid</strong> family makes red cells, platelets and most white cells. The <strong>lymphoid</strong> family makes lymphocytes, the immune system's specialists. A blood cancer is named after the branch where the broken cell sits:</p><p><strong>Leukaemia</strong> ("white blood"): immature cells called blasts flood the marrow and spill into the blood. Acute types (AML, ALL) grow in days to weeks; chronic types (CML, CLL) over years. <strong>Lymphoma</strong>: lymphocytes multiply into lumps in lymph nodes and the spleen. <strong>Myeloma</strong>: plasma cells, the antibody factories, pile up in the marrow and eat into bone.</p></div>

r="2"/></g><circle class="field-ring" cx="190" cy="132" r="115"/><circle class="field-ring" cx="570" cy="132" r="115"/><text class="t lg" x="190" y="272" text-anchor="middle">Normal blood</text><text In acute leukaemia, immature white cells called blasts crowd out everything else. A simple blood count is often what raises the alarm.</figcaption></figure>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">2 · How it starts</span><h3 class="sec">The hits that turn a cell</h3></div>
  <div class="pair">
    <div class="prose"><p>Blood cancers are often driven by <strong>chromosome swaps</strong> rather than single-letter typos. The famous example is the Philadelphia chromosome: pieces of chromosomes 9 and 22 trade places and fuse two genes into one, <em>BCR-ABL</em>, an accelerator jammed permanently on. It causes chronic myeloid leukaemia (CML).</p><p>In 2001 a pill called imatinib, built to block exactly that fused protein, turned CML from a fatal disease into one most patients live with for decades. It became the model for every targeted cancer drug since.</p><p>Why they make you ill: abnormal cells crowd the marrow, so it stops making enough healthy cells. Too few red cells means tiredness and paleness; too few platelets means bruising and bleeding; too few working white cells means infections that will not go away.</p></div>
    <aside class="note"><b>The usual hits</b><ul class="hits"><li><b>BCR-ABL</b><span>fused accelerator from the Philadelphia chromosome (CML, some ALL)</span></li><li><b>FLT3, NPM1</b><span>common drivers in acute myeloid leukaemia</span></li><li><b>MYC swap</b><span>accelerator moved next to an antibody gene, in Burkitt lymphoma</span></li><li><b>Epstein-Barr virus</b><span>helps drive some lymphomas</span></li></ul></aside>
  </div>
  <div class="risk">
    <div class="fix"><h4>Risks you cannot change</h4><ul><li>Age (most types), though ALL peaks in young children</li><li>Being male</li><li>Down syndrome and some inherited conditions</li><li>Earlier chemotherapy or radiation</li></ul></div>
    <div class="chg"><h4>Risks you can change</h4><ul><li>Smoking (raises AML risk)</li><li>Benzene exposure (petrol fumes, some industries)</li><li>HIV and other causes of a weak immune system</li><li>Some infections: EBV, H. pylori, HTLV-1</li></ul></div>
  </div>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">3 · How it grows</span><h3 class="sec">What the crowding does</h3></div>
  <div class="stages"><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 100%, var(--eosin))"><b>Marrow</b><span>Abnormal cells take over the marrow</span></div><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 75%, var(--eosin))"><b>Red cells ↓</b><span>Anaemia: tiredness, paleness, breathlessness</span></div><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 50%, var(--eosin))"><b>Platelets ↓</b><span>Easy bruising, nosebleeds, bleeding gums, tiny red spots</span></div><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 25%, var(--eosin))"><b>White cells ✗</b><span>Fevers and infections that keep coming back</span></div><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 0%, var(--eosin))"><b>Organs</b><span>Swollen nodes, spleen or liver; bone pain in myeloma</span></div></div>
  <figure class="plate"><div class="fig-tag"><b>Survival</b>5-year relative survival by type</div>
<div class="surv"><div class="srow"><div class="lab"><b>Lymphoma (non-Hodgkin)</b><span>all stages</span></div><div class="track"><div class="fill" style="width:74.3%;background:var(--teal)"></div><em style="left:calc(74.3% - 10px);transform:translate(-100%,-50%);color:var(--paper)">74.3%</em></div></div><div class="srow"><div class="lab"><b>Leukaemia</b><span>all types</span></div><div class="track"><div class="fill" style="width:68.6%;background:var(--teal)"></div><em style="left:calc(68.6% - 10px);transform:translate(-100%,-50%);color:var(--paper)">68.6%</em></div></div><div class="srow"><div class="lab"><b>Myeloma</b><span>all stages</span></div><div class="track"><div class="fill" style="width:63.7%;background:var(--teal)"></div><em style="left:calc(63.7% - 10px);transform:translate(-100%,-50%);color:var(--paper)">63.7%</em></div></div></div>
<figcaption>Share of people alive five years after diagnosis, compared with people of the same age without cancer. US SEER data, diagnosed 2016 to 2022.  Survival in India is lower, mostly because diagnosis comes later.</figcaption></figure>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">4 · Where it travels</span><h3 class="sec">Where it lives</h3></div>
  <div class="spread">
    <svg viewBox="10 0 180 420" role="img" aria-label="Body map: starts in the bone marrow, and most often spreads to bone marrow, neck nodes, armpit nodes, groin nodes, spleen."><defs><marker id="ablood" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="8" refY="5" markerWidth="7" markerHeight="7" orient="auto-start-reverse"><path class="mk" d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z"/></marker><marker id="bblood" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="8" refY="5" markerWidth="7" markerHeight="7" orient="auto-start-reverse"><path class="mk bad" d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z"/></marker></defs><path class="bm-body" d="M88,60 L112,60 L114,72 C140,74 156,82 160,98 L172,190 C174,200 170,206 164,204 L150,128 L146,200 L150,250 L144,402 C143,410 128,410 127,402 L108,262 L92,262 L73,402 C72,410 57,410 56,402 L50,250 L54,200 L50,128 L36,204 C30,206 26,200 28,190 L40,98 C44,82 60,74 86,72 Z"/><circle class="bm-body" cx="100" cy="36" r="24"/><ellipse class="bm-organ" cx="85" cy="112" rx="13" ry="25"/><ellipse class="bm-organ" cx="115" cy="112" rx="13" ry="25"/><path class="bm-organ" d="M70,142 C80,136 104,136 108,142 C108,152 96,160 76,158 C70,154 68,148 70,142 Z"/><ellipse class="bm-organ" cx="116" cy="148" rx="11" ry="8" transform="rotate(-20 116 148)"/><rect class="bm-organ line-only" x="78" y="168" width="44" height="34" rx="12"/><ellipse class="bm-organ" cx="100" cy="218" rx="9" ry="7"/><line class="bm-bone" x1="70" y1="262" x2="66" y2="360"/><line class="bm-bone" x1="130" y1="262" x2="134" y2="360"/><line class="bm-bone" x1="100" y1="76" x2="100" y2="250"/><path class="spread-line" d="M68,305 Q86.0,305.0 68,305" marker-end="url(#bblood)"/><path class="spread-line" d="M68,305 Q66.0,186.5 100,68" marker-end="url(#bblood)"/><path class="spread-line" d="M68,305 Q84.0,203.5 64,102" marker-end="url(#bblood)"/><path class="spread-line" d="M68,305 Q56.0,278.5 80,252" marker-end="url(#bblood)"/><path class="spread-line" d="M68,305 Q80.0,222.5 128,140" marker-end="url(#bblood)"/><circle class="spread-dot" cx="68" cy="305" r="5"/><circle class="spread-dot" cx="100" cy="68" r="5"/><circle class="spread-dot" cx="64" cy="102" r="5"/><circle class="spread-dot" cx="80" cy="252" r="5"/><circle class="spread-dot" cx="128" cy="140" r="5"/><circle class="primary-dot pulse" cx="68" cy="305" r="8"/></svg>
    <div class="prose"><p>Blood cancers do not spread the way solid tumors do; they live in the marrow, blood, lymph nodes and spleen from the start. Lymphomas are staged by how many node areas are involved, on one or both sides of the diaphragm.</p><ul><li class="p">starts: bone marrow</li><li>bone marrow</li><li>neck nodes</li><li>armpit nodes</li><li>groin nodes</li><li>spleen</li></ul></div>
  </div>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">5 · Catching it</span><h3 class="sec">Signs and screening</h3></div>
  <div class="two">
    <div class="box-l"><h4>Signs to get checked</h4><ul><li>Tiredness and paleness that do not improve</li><li>Easy bruising, bleeding gums, tiny red dots on the skin</li><li>Frequent fevers or infections</li><li>Painless swollen lymph nodes in the neck, armpit or groin</li><li>Drenching night sweats and weight loss</li><li>Bone or back pain (myeloma)</li></ul></div>
    <div class="box-l ok"><h4>Prevent it, or find it early</h4><ul><li>There is no routine screening test</li><li>Many cases are first spotted on an ordinary blood count (CBC)</li><li>Avoid tobacco and benzene exposure</li><li>Most blood cancers cannot be prevented, so knowing the signs matters</li></ul></div>
  </div>
  <div class="myth"><span class="k kq">Myth</span><p class="mq">Blood cancer is a death sentence.</p><span class="k kf">Fact</span><p>Many types are very treatable. In well-resourced settings about 9 in 10 children with ALL are cured, and CML is controlled for decades with a daily pill.</p></div>
  <div class="pn"><a href="#colorectal">← Colorectal cancer</a><a href="#stomach">Stomach cancer →</a></div>
</section>
<section class="view" id="v-stomach" data-view="stomach" hidden>
  <header class="c-head">
    <p class="eyebrow">Chapter 7 of 10 · starts in the gland cells lining the stomach</p>
    <h2>Stomach cancer</h2>
    <p class="thesis">A slow cancer most often set in motion by a stomach bacterium, H. pylori. It hides behind symptoms that feel like ordinary acidity, so it is usually found late.</p>
  </header>
  <div class="stats"><div class="stat"><b>970,000</b><span>new cases a year, #5 worldwide</span></div><div class="stat"><b>#4 in Indian men</b><span>more common in the north-east</span></div><div class="stat"><b>78%</b><span>5-year survival if found early, vs 8% after spread</span></div></div>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">1 · Where it starts</span><h3 class="sec">The cells at the root</h3></div>
  <div class="prose"><p>The stomach wall has layers: an inner lining full of glands that make acid and digestive juices, then connective tissue, muscle and an outer skin.</p><p>About 9 in 10 stomach cancers are adenocarcinomas that start in the gland cells of the inner lining, most often in the lower part of the stomach, the antrum.</p></div>
  <figure class="plate"><div class="fig-tag"><b>Fig 1</b>Where it starts</div><div class="scroll" class="minw-600"><svg viewBox="0 0 760 334" role="img" aria-label="The stomach with corkscrew H. pylori bacteria in its lining and a marker at the lower stomach. x="650" y="258" text-anchor="middle">invades the wall</text><line class="line" x1="552" y1="56" x2="566" y2="56" marker-end="url(#ast)"/><line class="line" x1="650" y1="82" x2="650" y2="122" marker-end="url(#ast)"/><line class="line" x1="568" y1="150" x2="554" y2="150" marker-end="url(#ast)"/><line class="line" x1="470" y1="176" x2="470" y2="216" marker-end="url(#ast)"/><line class="line text-anchor="middle">takes decades · salt-preserved food and smoking speed it up</text><text class="s" x="560" y="316" text-anchor="middle">· clearing H. pylori with antibiotics can stop it early</text></svg></div><figcaption>H. pylori sets off a decades-long chain. Each box is a stage a doctor can see on a biopsy, and treating the infection early can stop the chain.</figcaption></figure>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">2 · How it starts</span><h3 class="sec">The hits that turn a cell</h3></div>
  <div class="pair">
    <div class="prose"><p><strong>H. pylori</strong> is a corkscrew-shaped bacterium that burrows into the stomach's mucus layer, usually caught in childhood. A large share of the world's population carries it, and most never get cancer. But in some people it keeps the lining inflamed for decades.</p><p>That inflammation sets off a slow cascade: chronic gastritis, loss of the acid glands (atrophy), the lining turning into intestine-like tissue (intestinal metaplasia), precancer (dysplasia), and finally cancer. Salt-preserved and smoked foods and smoking speed it up; fresh fruit and vegetables slow it. The spread of refrigerators, which cut the need for salting food, is one reason stomach cancer has become rarer worldwide.</p><p>A second, "diffuse" type spreads through the wall as scattered single cells. Some families carry an inherited fault in the <em>CDH1</em> gene that causes it.</p></div>
    <aside class="note"><b>The usual hits</b><ul class="hits"><li><b>CDH1</b><span>the cell-glue gene; when lost, cells drift apart and spread through the wall</span></li><li><b>TP53</b><span>the master brake</span></li><li><b>HER2</b><span>accelerator in some tumors, blocked by targeted drugs</span></li><li><b>Epstein-Barr virus</b><span>found in about 1 in 10 stomach cancers</span></li></ul></aside>
  </div>
  <div class="risk">
    <div class="fix"><h4>Risks you cannot change</h4><ul><li>Age</li><li>Being male</li><li>Family history, or inherited CDH1</li><li>Blood group A (slightly higher risk)</li></ul></div>
    <div class="chg"><h4>Risks you can change</h4><ul><li>H. pylori infection (treatable with antibiotics)</li><li>Salted, pickled and smoked foods</li><li>Smoking</li><li>Few fresh fruits and vegetables</li></ul></div>
  </div>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">3 · How it grows</span><h3 class="sec">Stages, from earliest to most advanced</h3></div>
  <div class="stages"><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 100%, var(--eosin))"><b>Stage I</b><span>In the lining or just below, at most a few nodes</span></div><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 67%, var(--eosin))"><b>Stage II</b><span>Into the muscle, or more nodes</span></div><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 33%, var(--eosin))"><b>Stage III</b><span>Through the wall, many nodes</span></div><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 0%, var(--eosin))"><b>Stage IV</b><span>Liver, belly lining or far organs</span></div></div>
  <figure class="plate"><div class="fig-tag"><b>Survival</b>5-year relative survival by how far it has spread</div>
<div class="surv"><div class="srow"><div class="lab"><b>Localized</b><span>only in the stomach</span></div><div class="track"><div class="fill" style="width:78.1%;background:var(--teal)"></div><em style="left:calc(78.1% - 10px);transform:translate(-100%,-50%);color:var(--paper)">78.1%</em></div></div><div class="srow"><div class="lab"><b>Regional</b><span>nearby nodes or organs</span></div><div class="track"><div class="fill" style="width:39.0%;background:var(--hema)"></div><em style="left:calc(39.0% - 10px);transform:translate(-100%,-50%);color:var(--paper)">39%</em></div></div><div class="srow"><div class="lab"><b>Distant</b><span>spread to far organs</span></div><div class="track"><div class="fill" style="width:8.1%;background:var(--eosin)"></div><em style="left:calc(8.1% + 8px);color:var(--ink)">8.1%</em></div></div></div>
<figcaption>Share of people alive five years after diagnosis, compared with people of the same age without cancer. US SEER data, diagnosed 2016 to 2022.  Survival in India is lower, mostly because diagnosis comes later.</figcaption></figure>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">4 · Where it travels</span><h3 class="sec">How it spreads</h3></div>
  <div class="spread">
    <svg viewBox="10 0 180 420" role="img" aria-label="Body map: starts in the stomach, and most often spreads to liver, belly lining, neck nodes, lungs."><defs><marker id="astomach" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="8" refY="5" markerWidth="7" markerHeight="7" orient="auto-start-reverse"><path class="mk" d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z"/></marker><marker id="bstomach" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="8" refY="5" markerWidth="7" markerHeight="7" orient="auto-start-reverse"><path class="mk bad" d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z"/></marker></defs><path class="bm-body" d="M88,60 L112,60 L114,72 C140,74 156,82 160,98 L172,190 C174,200 170,206 164,204 L150,128 L146,200 L150,250 L144,402 C143,410 128,410 127,402 L108,262 L92,262 L73,402 C72,410 57,410 56,402 L50,250 L54,200 L50,128 L36,204 C30,206 26,200 28,190 L40,98 C44,82 60,74 86,72 Z"/><circle class="bm-body" cx="100" cy="36" r="24"/><ellipse class="bm-organ" cx="85" cy="112" rx="13" ry="25"/><ellipse class="bm-organ" cx="115" cy="112" rx="13" ry="25"/><path class="bm-organ" d="M70,142 C80,136 104,136 108,142 C108,152 96,160 76,158 C70,154 68,148 70,142 Z"/><ellipse class="bm-organ" cx="116" cy="148" rx="11" ry="8" transform="rotate(-20 116 148)"/><rect class="bm-organ line-only" x="78" y="168" width="44" height="34" rx="12"/><ellipse class="bm-organ" cx="100" cy="218" rx="9" ry="7"/><line class="bm-bone" x1="70" y1="262" x2="66" y2="360"/><line class="bm-bone" x1="130" y1="262" x2="134" y2="360"/><line class="bm-bone" x1="100" y1="76" x2="100" y2="250"/><path class="spread-line" d="M116,148 Q119.0,148.0 86,148" marker-end="url(#bstomach)"/><path class="spread-line" d="M116,148 Q131.0,173.0 110,198" marker-end="url(#bstomach)"/><path class="spread-line" d="M116,148 Q126.0,108.0 100,68" marker-end="url(#bstomach)"/><path class="spread-line" d="M116,148 Q133.5,128.0 115,108" marker-end="url(#bstomach)"/><circle class="spread-dot" cx="86" cy="148" r="5"/><circle class="spread-dot" cx="110" cy="198" r="5"/><circle class="spread-dot" cx="100" cy="68" r="5"/><circle class="spread-dot" cx="115" cy="108" r="5"/><circle class="primary-dot pulse" cx="116" cy="148" r="8"/></svg>
    <div class="prose"><p>It spreads to the liver, the lining of the belly, and nodes. A hard node above the left collarbone, called Virchow's node, is a classic sign doctors look for.</p><ul><li class="p">starts: stomach</li><li>liver</li><li>belly lining</li><li>neck nodes</li><li>lungs</li></ul></div>
  </div>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">5 · Catching it</span><h3 class="sec">Signs and screening</h3></div>
  <div class="two">
    <div class="box-l"><h4>Signs to get checked</h4><ul><li>Indigestion or heartburn that does not go away</li><li>Feeling full after small meals</li><li>Upper belly pain</li><li>Nausea or vomiting, sometimes with blood</li><li>Black, tarry stools</li><li>Weight loss and anaemia</li></ul></div>
    <div class="box-l ok"><h4>Prevent it, or find it early</h4><ul><li>No routine screening in India; Japan and Korea screen with endoscopy because rates there are high</li><li>Get tested and treated for H. pylori if you have ulcers or a family history</li><li>Persistent new indigestion after 50 deserves an endoscopy</li><li>Less salted and smoked food, more fresh produce, no tobacco</li></ul></div>
  </div>
  <div class="myth"><span class="k kq">Myth</span><p class="mq">Spicy food causes stomach cancer.</p><span class="k kf">Fact</span><p>There is no strong evidence that chilli causes it. H. pylori, salt-preserved food and smoking are the real culprits.</p></div>
  <div class="pn"><a href="#blood">← Blood cancers</a><a href="#liver">Liver cancer →</a></div>
</section>
<section class="view" id="v-liver" data-view="liver" hidden>
  <header class="c-head">
    <p class="eyebrow">Chapter 8 of 10 · starts in the liver's main working cells, the hepatocytes</p>
    <h2>Liver cancer</h2>
    <p class="thesis">Almost always the end of a long road of liver damage. Hepatitis viruses, alcohol and fatty liver scar the liver over decades, and cancer grows in the scarred ground.</p>
  </header>
  <div class="stats"><div class="stat"><b>865,000</b><span>new cases a year; #3 cause of cancer death worldwide</span></div><div class="stat"><b>39,000 deaths</b><span>a year in India</span></div><div class="stat"><b>37%</b><span>5-year survival if found early, vs 4% after spread</span></div></div>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">1 · Where it starts</span><h3 class="sec">The cells at the root</h3></div>
  <div class="prose"><p>The liver is the body's chemical factory, built from plates of cells called <em class="term">hepatocytes</em>. About 8 in 10 primary liver cancers are hepatocellular carcinoma (HCC), which starts in these cells.</p><p>Note: most cancer found in the liver has spread there from somewhere else, such as the bowel. That is not liver cancer; it is named after where it began.</p></div>
  <figure class="plate"><div class="fig-tag"><b>Fig 1</b>Where it starts</div><div class="scroll" class="minw-600"><svg viewBox="0 0 760 300" role="img" aria-label="Five livers in a row: healthy; fatty and inflamed; fibrosis with scar lines; cirrhosis covered in nodules; and cirrhosis with a cancer nodule. Causes listed above: hepatitis B, hepatitis C, alcohol, fatty liver and aflatoxin. The path usually takes 20 to 40 years."><defs><marker id="alv" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="8" refY="5" markerWidth="7" markerHeight="7" orient="auto-start-reverse"><path class="mk" d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z"/></marker><marker id="blv" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="8" refY="5" markerWidth="7" markerHeight="7" orient="auto-start-reverse"><path class="mk bad" d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10
