cycle of injury, death and regrowth is a chance for mutations. Cirrhosis is the scarred ground where most liver cancers grow.</figcaption></figure>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">2 · How it starts</span><h3 class="sec">The hits that turn a cell</h3></div>
  <div class="pair">
    <div class="prose"><p>Healthy liver cells rarely divide. But every time the liver is injured, cells die and the survivors divide to replace them. Years of injury lead to inflammation, then scar tissue (<strong>fibrosis</strong>), then <strong>cirrhosis</strong>, where the liver is broken into hard lumps surrounded by scar. Most liver cancers grow in a cirrhotic liver.</p><p>Hepatitis B can go further and insert its own DNA into the liver cell's genome. And <strong>aflatoxin</strong>, a toxin from mould on badly stored groundnuts, maize and grains, causes one very specific typo in the <em>TP53</em> brake gene, a fingerprint scientists can read in the tumor.</p></div>
    <aside class="note"><b>The usual hits</b><ul class="hits"><li><b>TERT</b><span>switched on to make cells immortal, in most cases</span></li><li><b>CTNNB1 (β-catenin)</b><span>accelerator for liver growth</span></li><li><b>TP53</b><span>brake; the R249S typo is the aflatoxin fingerprint</span></li><li><b>Hepatitis B DNA</b><span>viral DNA stitched into the genome</span></li></ul></aside>
  </div>
  <div class="risk">
    <div class="fix"><h4>Risks you cannot change</h4><ul><li>Being male</li><li>Age</li><li>Family history</li><li>Inherited iron overload</li></ul></div>
    <div class="chg"><h4>Risks you can change</h4><ul><li>Hepatitis B (vaccine) and hepatitis C (curable with pills)</li><li>Alcohol</li><li>Fatty liver from obesity and diabetes</li><li>Aflatoxin in mouldy grains and nuts</li><li>Smoking</li></ul></div>
  </div>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">3 · How it grows</span><h3 class="sec">Stages, from earliest to most advanced</h3></div>
  <div class="stages"><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 100%, var(--eosin))"><b>Stage I</b><span>One tumor, not in blood vessels</span></div><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 67%, var(--eosin))"><b>Stage II</b><span>One tumor in vessels, or several small ones</span></div><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 33%, var(--eosin))"><b>Stage III</b><span>Several large tumors, or in a major vein</span></div><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 0%, var(--eosin))"><b>Stage IV</b><span>Lymph nodes or far organs</span></div></div>
  <figure class="plate"><div class="fig-tag"><b>Survival</b>5-year relative survival by how far it has spread</div>
<div class="surv"><div class="srow"><div class="lab"><b>Localized</b><span>only in the liver</span></div><div class="track"><div class="fill" style="width:37.4%;background:var(--teal)"></div><em style="left:calc(37.4% - 10px);transform:translate(-100%,-50%);color:var(--paper)">37.4%</em></div></div><div class="srow"><div class="lab"><b>Regional</b><span>nearby nodes or vessels</span></div><div class="track"><div class="fill" style="width:13.4%;background:var(--hema)"></div><em style="left:calc(13.4% + 8px);color:var(--ink)">13.4%</em></div></div><div class="srow"><div class="lab"><b>Distant</b><span>spread to far organs</span></div><div class="track"><div class="fill" style="width:3.6%;background:var(--eosin)"></div><em style="left:calc(3.6% + 8px);color:var(--ink)">3.6%</em></div></div></div>
<figcaption>Share of people alive five years after diagnosis, compared with people of the same age without cancer. US SEER data, diagnosed 2016 to 2022.  Survival in India is lower, mostly because diagnosis comes later.</figcaption></figure>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">4 · Where it travels</span><h3 class="sec">How it spreads</h3></div>
  <div class="spread">
    <svg viewBox="10 0 180 420" role="img" aria-label="Body map: starts in the liver, and most often spreads to lungs, spine &amp; pelvis, neck nodes."><defs><marker id="aliver" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="8" refY="5" markerWidth="7" markerHeight="7" orient="auto-start-reverse"><path class="mk" d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z"/></marker><marker id="bliver" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="8" refY="5" markerWidth="7" markerHeight="7" orient="auto-start-reverse"><path class="mk bad" d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z"/></marker></defs><path class="bm-body" d="M88,60 L112,60 L114,72 C140,74 156,82 160,98 L172,190 C174,200 170,206 164,204 L150,128 L146,200 L150,250 L144,402 C143,410 128,410 127,402 L108,262 L92,262 L73,402 C72,410 57,410 56,402 L50,250 L54,200 L50,128 L36,204 C30,206 26,200 28,190 L40,98 C44,82 60,74 86,72 Z"/><circle class="bm-body" cx="100" cy="36" r="24"/><ellipse class="bm-organ" cx="85" cy="112" rx="13" ry="25"/><ellipse class="bm-organ" cx="115" cy="112" rx="13" ry="25"/><path class="bm-organ" d="M70,142 C80,136 104,136 108,142 C108,152 96,160 76,158 C70,154 68,148 70,142 Z"/><ellipse class="bm-organ" cx="116" cy="148" rx="11" ry="8" transform="rotate(-20 116 148)"/><rect class="bm-organ line-only" x="78" y="168" width="44" height="34" rx="12"/><ellipse class="bm-organ" cx="100" cy="218" rx="9" ry="7"/><line class="bm-bone" x1="70" y1="262" x2="66" y2="360"/><line class="bm-bone" x1="130" y1="262" x2="134" y2="360"/><line class="bm-bone" x1="100" y1="76" x2="100" y2="250"/><path class="spread-line" d="M86,148 Q82.5,128.0 115,108" marker-end="url(#bliver)"/><path class="spread-line" d="M86,148 Q75.0,192.0 100,236" marker-end="url(#bliver)"/><path class="spread-line" d="M86,148 Q75.0,108.0 100,68" marker-end="url(#bliver)"/><circle class="spread-dot" cx="115" cy="108" r="5"/><circle class="spread-dot" cx="100" cy="236" r="5"/><circle class="spread-dot" cx="100" cy="68" r="5"/><circle class="primary-dot pulse" cx="86" cy="148" r="8"/></svg>
    <div class="prose"><p>It tends to grow inside the liver and into its veins. When it spreads further, the lungs and bones are the most common targets.</p><ul><li class="p">starts: liver</li><li>lungs</li><li>spine &amp; pelvis</li><li>neck nodes</li></ul></div>
  </div>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">5 · Catching it</span><h3 class="sec">Signs and screening</h3></div>
  <div class="two">
    <div class="box-l"><h4>Signs to get checked</h4><ul><li>Often none until late</li><li>Pain or a lump in the upper right belly</li><li>Swollen belly from fluid</li><li>Yellow skin or eyes (jaundice)</li><li>Weight loss and feeling full quickly</li><li>Sudden worsening in someone who already has cirrhosis</li></ul></div>
    <div class="box-l ok"><h4>Prevent it, or find it early</h4><ul><li>Ultrasound every 6 months, often with an AFP blood test, for people with cirrhosis or long-term hepatitis B</li><li>Hepatitis B vaccine at birth</li><li>Get tested for hepatitis C; pills cure it in a few months</li><li>Limit alcohol, keep a healthy weight, store grains and nuts dry</li></ul></div>
  </div>
  <div class="myth"><span class="k kq">Myth</span><p class="mq">Only heavy drinkers get liver cancer.</p><span class="k kf">Fact</span><p>Worldwide, hepatitis B and C cause most liver cancer, and fatty liver from obesity and diabetes is a fast-growing cause.</p></div>
  <div class="pn"><a href="#stomach">← Stomach cancer</a><a href="#prostate">Prostate cancer →</a></div>
</section>
<section class="view" id="v-prostate" data-view="prostate" hidden>
  <header class="c-head">
    <p class="eyebrow">Chapter 9 of 10 · starts in the gland cells of the prostate</p>
    <h2>Prostate cancer</h2>
    <p class="thesis">Very common in older men and often slow. Many prostate cancers would never cause harm, so the hard part is telling the dangerous ones from the lazy ones.</p>
  </header>
  <div class="stats"><div class="stat"><b>1.5 million</b><span>new cases a year, #2 in men worldwide</span></div><div class="stat"><b>#5 in Indian men</b><span>much less common than in the West</span></div><div class="stat"><b>~100%</b><span>5-year survival unless it spreads, then 40%</span></div></div>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">1 · Where it starts</span><h3 class="sec">The cells at the root</h3></div>
  <div class="prose"><p>The prostate is a walnut-sized gland below the bladder that makes part of the fluid in semen. The urethra, the tube that carries urine, runs straight through it.</p><p>About 7 in 10 cancers start in the <em class="term">peripheral zone</em> at the back, next to the rectum, which is why a doctor can feel them with a finger. Benign enlargement (BPH), the cause of the familiar urinary trouble in older men, grows in the transition zone around the urethra. So early cancer usually causes no symptoms at all.</p></div>

text-anchor="middle">Group 1</text><text class="s" x="510" y="329" text-anchor="middle">neat glands, slow</text><text class="t b" x="610" y="314" text-anchor="middle">Group 3</text><text class="s" x="610" y="329" text-anchor="middle">crowded glands</text><text class="t b red" x="710" y="314" text-anchor="middle">Group 5</text><text class="s" x="710" y="329" text-anchor="middle">sheets of cells</text></svg></div><figcaption>Left: the prostate sits under the bladder, wrapped around the urethra, with the rectum just behind. Right: pathologists grade prostate cancer by how much the glands have lost their shape.</figcaption></figure>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">2 · How it starts</span><h3 class="sec">The hits that turn a cell</h3></div>
  <div class="pair">
    <div class="prose"><p>Prostate cells run on male hormones (androgens). Cancer cells keep this dependence, which is why therapy that lowers testosterone shrinks them, often for years.</p><p>In about half of cases in Western studies, two genes fuse (<em>TMPRSS2-ERG</em>) so that the hormone signal switches on a growth gene. Autopsy studies find small prostate cancers in many men over 70 who died of something else, which shows how often it stays harmless.</p><p>Pathologists judge how dangerous it is by the pattern of the glands, the <strong>Gleason score</strong>, now reported as grade groups 1 to 5.</p></div>
    <aside class="note"><b>The usual hits</b><ul class="hits"><li><b>TMPRSS2-ERG</b><span>fusion that hands a growth gene to the hormone switch</span></li><li><b>PTEN</b><span>a brake, lost in more aggressive tumors</span></li><li><b>BRCA2</b><span>inherited repair gene fault; raises risk and aggressiveness</span></li><li><b>Androgen receptor</b><span>the hormone switch that later tumors learn to bypass</span></li></ul></aside>
  </div>
  <div class="risk">
    <div class="fix"><h4>Risks you cannot change</h4><ul><li>Age: rare before 50</li><li>Family history</li><li>African ancestry</li><li>Inherited BRCA2</li></ul></div>
    <div class="chg"><h4>Risks you can change</h4><ul><li>Little is proven</li><li>Staying at a healthy weight may lower the risk of aggressive cancer</li></ul></div>
  </div>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">3 · How it grows</span><h3 class="sec">Stages, from earliest to most advanced</h3></div>
  <div class="stages"><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 100%, var(--eosin))"><b>Stage I</b><span>Small, slow, felt in one area or found by blood test</span></div><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 67%, var(--eosin))"><b>Stage II</b><span>Still inside the gland, higher grade</span></div><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 33%, var(--eosin))"><b>Stage III</b><span>Through the gland's capsule into nearby tissue</span></div><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 0%, var(--eosin))"><b>Stage IV</b><span>Lymph nodes, bones or far organs</span></div></div>
  <figure class="plate"><div class="fig-tag"><b>Survival</b>5-year relative survival by how far it has spread</div>
<div class="surv"><div class="srow"><div class="lab"><b>Localized</b><span>inside the prostate</span></div><div class="track"><div class="fill" style="width:100.0%;background:var(--teal)"></div><em style="left:calc(100.0% - 10px);transform:translate(-100%,-50%);color:var(--paper)">100%</em></div></div><div class="srow"><div class="lab"><b>Regional</b><span>nearby tissue or nodes</span></div><div class="track"><div class="fill" style="width:100.0%;background:var(--hema)"></div><em style="left:calc(100.0% - 10px);transform:translate(-100%,-50%);color:var(--paper)">100%</em></div></div><div class="srow"><div class="lab"><b>Distant</b><span>spread to bone or far organs</span></div><div class="track"><div class="fill" style="width:40.1%;background:var(--eosin)"></div><em style="left:calc(40.1% - 10px);transform:translate(-100%,-50%);color:var(--paper)">40.1%</em></div></div></div>
<figcaption>Share of people alive five years after diagnosis, compared with people of the same age without cancer. US SEER data, diagnosed 2016 to 2022.  Survival in India is lower, mostly because diagnosis comes later.</figcaption></figure>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">4 · Where it travels</span><h3 class="sec">How it spreads</h3></div>
  <div class="spread">
    <svg viewBox="10 0 180 420" role="img" aria-label="Body map: starts in the prostate, and most often spreads to spine &amp; pelvis, pelvic nodes, lungs, liver."><defs><marker id="aprostate" marker-end="url(#bprostate)"/><path class="spread-line" d="M104,230 Q114.0,220.0 88,210" marker-end="url(#bprostate)"/><path class="spread-line" d="M104,230 Q91.5,169.0 115,108" marker-end="url(#bprostate)"/><path class="spread-line" d="M104,230 Q113.0,189.0 86,148" marker-end="url(#bprostate)"/><circle class="spread-dot" cx="100" cy="236" r="5"/><circle class="spread-dot"     <div class="prose"><p>It has a strong preference for bone, especially the spine and pelvis. Back or hip pain can be the first sign of advanced disease.</p><ul><li class="p">starts: prostate</li><li>spine &amp; pelvis</li><li>pelvic nodes</li><li>lungs</li><li>liver</li></ul></div>
  </div>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">5 · Catching it</span><h3 class="sec">Signs and screening</h3></div>
  <div class="two">
    <div class="box-l"><h4>Signs to get checked</h4><ul><li>Usually none early</li><li>Weak or interrupted urine flow, or getting up at night to pass urine (far more often caused by BPH)</li><li>Blood in urine or semen</li><li>Pain in the back, hips or pelvis</li></ul></div>
    <div class="box-l ok"><h4>Prevent it, or find it early</h4><ul><li>PSA blood test: men aged 55 to 69 should discuss it with a doctor (US guideline)</li><li>Earlier talk if you have family history or BRCA2</li><li>Low-risk cancers are often watched with regular tests (active surveillance) instead of treated</li><li>Treatment can affect urine control and sexual function, so the choice is personal</li></ul></div>
  </div>
  <div class="myth"><span class="k kq">Myth</span><p class="mq">A high PSA means you have cancer.</p><span class="k kf">Fact</span><p>PSA also rises with benign enlargement, infection, and even recent cycling. It is a prompt for further tests, not a diagnosis.</p></div>
  <div class="pn"><a href="#liver">← Liver cancer</a><a href="#skin">Skin cancer (melanoma) →</a></div>
</section>
<section class="view" id="v-skin" data-view="skin" hidden>
  <header class="c-head">
    <p class="eyebrow">Chapter 10 of 10 · starts in the pigment cells of the skin, the melanocytes</p>
    <h2>Skin cancer (melanoma)</h2>
    <p class="thesis">Melanoma is the dangerous skin cancer: a cancer of pigment cells that can spread early. It is rare in India, but when it happens here it is often on the palms, soles or nails, and found late.</p>
  </header>
  <div class="stats"><div class="stat"><b>332,000</b><span>new melanomas a year worldwide</span></div><div class="stat"><b>Rare in India</b><span>about 1,500 deaths a year</span></div><div class="stat"><b>100%</b><span>5-year survival if found early, vs 34% after spread</span></div></div>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">1 · Where it starts</span><h3 class="sec">The cells at the root</h3></div>
  <div class="prose"><p>The outer layer of skin, the epidermis, is built from flat cells called keratinocytes. Scattered along its base are <em class="term">melanocytes</em>, which make the pigment melanin and hand it to their neighbours as a sun umbrella over their DNA.</p><p>There are three main skin cancers. <strong>Basal cell</strong> is the most common and almost never spreads. <strong>Squamous cell</strong> occasionally spreads. <strong>Melanoma</strong>, from melanocytes, is the least common but causes most skin cancer deaths.</p></div>
  <figure class="plate"><div class="fig-tag"><b>Fig 1</b>Where it starts</div><div class="scroll" class="minw-600"><svg viewBox="0 0 760 312" role="img" aria-label="Cross-section of skin: UV light hits the epidermis; pigment cells (melanocytes) sit along its base. A harmless mole is a cluster of them. A melanoma spreads along the base then grows down into the dermis toward a blood vessel. A ruler shows depth in millimetres, the Breslow thickness."><defs><marker id="ask" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="8" refY="5" markerWidth="7" markerHeight="7" orient="auto-start-reverse"><path class="mk" class="uv" x1="70" y1="6" x2="88" y2="46" marker-end="url(#bsk)"/><line class="uv" x1="110" y1="6" x2="128" y2="46" marker-end="url(#bsk)"/><line class="uv" x1="150" y1="6" x2="168" y2="46" marker-end="url(#bsk)"/><text class="s red" x="176" y="26">UV light fuses DNA letters</text><text class="s" x="80" y="140" text-anchor="middle">melanocytes</text><text class="s" x="80" y="154" text-anchor="middle">(pigment cells)</text><text class="s" x="248" y="140" text-anchor="middle">mole</text><text class="s" x="248" y="154" text-anchor="middle">(harmless cluster)</text><text class="t b red" x="415" y="46" text-anchor="middle">melanoma</text><text class="s" x="548" y="64">epidermis</text><text class="s" x="548" y="200">dermis</text><text class="s" x="548" y="225">blood vessel</text><text class="s" x="548" y="292">fat</text><line class="ruler" x1="530" y1="114" x2="530" y2="196"/><line class="ruler" x1="524" y1="114" x2="536" y2="114"/><line class="ruler" x1="524" y1="134" x2="536" y2="134"/><line class="ruler" x1="524" y1="155" x2="536" y2="155"/><line class="ruler" x1="524" y1="176" x2="536" y2="176"/><line class="ruler" x1="524" y1="196" x2="536" alone</text></svg></div><figcaption>A mole is a harmless cluster of pigment cells. Melanoma first spreads sideways along the base of the epidermis, then dives into the dermis, where it can reach blood and lymph vessels. Depth is what matters most.</figcaption></figure>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">2 · How it starts</span><h3 class="sec">The hits that turn a cell</h3></div>
  <div class="pair">
    <div class="prose"><p>Ultraviolet light fuses neighbouring DNA letters together; when the cell repairs the damage it often writes the wrong letter. Melanoma carries more mutations than almost any other cancer, most of them with this UV signature.</p><p>About half of melanomas carry the same typo in the <em>BRAF</em> accelerator gene (V600E). Drugs that block it, and immunotherapies that release the immune system's brakes, have transformed survival for advanced melanoma in the last 15 years.</p><p>Not all melanoma comes from the sun. <strong>Acral melanoma</strong>, on palms, soles and under nails, has little to do with UV. It is the most common type in people with darker skin, including in India.</p></div>
    <aside class="note"><b>The usual hits</b><ul class="hits"><li><b>BRAF V600E</b><span>accelerator stuck on in about half; blocked by targeted pills</span></li><li><b>NRAS</b><span>accelerator in about 1 in 5</span></li><li><b>CDKN2A</b><span>brake; inherited faults run in melanoma families</span></li><li><b>TERT</b><span>switched on to make cells immortal</span></li></ul></aside>
  </div>
  <div class="risk">
    <div class="fix"><h4>Risks you cannot change</h4><ul><li>Fair skin, light eyes, red or blond hair</li><li>Many moles (more than 50) or unusual moles</li><li>Family history</li><li>Weak immunity</li></ul></div>
    <div class="chg"><h4>Risks you can change</h4><ul><li>Sunburns, especially in childhood</li><li>Tanning beds</li><li>Unprotected time in strong sun</li></ul></div>
  </div>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">3 · How it grows</span><h3 class="sec">Stages, from earliest to most advanced</h3></div>
  <div class="stages"><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 100%, var(--eosin))"><b>Stage 0</b><span>Only in the top layer (in situ)</span></div><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 75%, var(--eosin))"><b>Stage I</b><span>Thin, up to about 1 to 2 mm, not ulcerated</span></div><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 50%, var(--eosin))"><b>Stage II</b><span>Thicker or ulcerated, no nodes</span></div><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 25%, var(--eosin))"><b>Stage III</b><span>Nearby lymph nodes or skin</span></div><div class="stg" style="border-top-color:color-mix(in oklab, var(--teal) 0%, var(--eosin))"><b>Stage IV</b><span>Lungs, liver, brain or other far organs</span></div></div>
  <figure class="plate"><div class="fig-tag"><b>Survival</b>5-year relative survival by how far it has spread</div>
<div class="surv"><div class="srow"><div class="lab"><b>Localized</b><span>only in the skin</span></div><div class="track"><div class="fill" style="width:100.0%;background:var(--teal)"></div><em style="left:calc(100.0% - 10px);transform:translate(-100%,-50%);color:var(--paper)">100%</em></div></div><div class="srow"><div class="lab"><b>Regional</b><span>reached nearby nodes</span></div><div class="track"><div class="fill" style="width:76.0%;background:var(--hema)"></div><em style="left:calc(76.0% - 10px);transform:translate(-100%,-50%);color:var(--paper)">76%</em></div></div><div class="srow"><div class="lab"><b>Distant</b><span>spread to far organs</span></div><div class="track"><div class="fill" style="width:34.0%;background:var(--eosin)"></div><em style="left:calc(34.0% - 10px);transform:translate(-100%,-50%);color:var(--paper)">34%</em></div></div></div>
<figcaption>Share of people alive five years after diagnosis, compared with people of the same age without cancer. US SEER data, diagnosed 2016 to 2022.  Survival in India is lower, mostly because diagnosis comes later.</figcaption></figure>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">4 · Where it travels</span><h3 class="sec">How it spreads</h3></div>
  <div class="spread">
    <svg viewBox="10 0 180 420" role="img" aria-label="Body map: starts in the skin, and most often spreads to armpit nodes, lungs, liver, brain."><defs><marker id="askin" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="8" class="bm-organ" d="M70,142 C80,136 104,136 108,142 C108,152 96,160 76,158 C70,154 68,148 70,142 Z"/><ellipse class="bm-organ" cx="116" cy="148" rx="11" ry="8" transform="rotate(-20 116 148)"/><rect class="bm-organ line-only" x="78" y="168" width="44" height="34" rx="12"/><ellipse class="bm-organ" cx="100" cy="218" rx="9" ry="7"/><line class="bm-bone" x1="70" y1="262" x2="66" y2="360"/><line class="bm-bone" x1="130" y1="262" x2="134" y2="360"/><line class="bm-bone" x1="100" y1="76" x2="100" y2="250"/><path class="spread-line" d="M160,165 Q130.0,133.5 64,102" marker-end="url(#bskin)"/><path class="spread-line" d="M160,165 Q155.5,136.5 115,108" marker-end="url(#bskin)"/><path class="spread-line" d="M160,165 Q141.0,156.5 86,148" marker-end="url(#bskin)"/><path class="spread-line" d="M160,165 Q148.0,97.5 100,30" marker-end="url(#bskin)"/><circle class="spread-dot" cx="64" cy="102" r="5"/><circle class="spread-dot" cx="115" cy="108" r="5"/><circle class="spread-dot" cx="86" cy="148" r="5"/><circle class="spread-dot" cx="100" cy="30" r="5"/><circle class="primary-dot pulse" cx="160" cy="165" r="8"/></svg>
    <div class="prose"><p>Once it grows down into the dermis it can reach lymph and blood vessels. It goes first to nearby nodes, then commonly to the lungs, liver and brain.</p><ul><li class="p">starts: skin</li><li>armpit nodes</li><li>lungs</li><li>liver</li><li>brain</li></ul></div>
  </div>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">5 · Catching it</span><h3 class="sec">Signs and screening</h3></div>
  <div class="two">
    <div class="box-l"><h4>Signs to get checked</h4><ul><li><strong>A</strong>symmetry: the two halves do not match</li><li><strong>B</strong>order: ragged, notched or blurred edge</li><li><strong>C</strong>olour: several shades of brown, black, red or blue</li><li><strong>D</strong>iameter: larger than 6 mm, about a pencil eraser</li><li><strong>E</strong>volving: changing in size, shape or colour</li><li>A new dark streak under a nail, or a dark spot on the sole that grows</li></ul></div>
    <div class="box-l ok"><h4>Prevent it, or find it early</h4><ul><li>Check your skin once a month, including palms, soles and nails</li><li>See a doctor about any mole that is new, changing or looks different from your others</li><li>Shade, clothing and sunscreen in strong sun; avoid sunburn</li><li>No tanning beds</li></ul></div>
  </div>
  <div class="myth"><span class="k kq">Myth</span><p class="mq">Dark skin cannot get skin cancer.</p><span class="k kf">Fact</span><p>It is less common, but melanoma of the palms, soles and nails occurs in every skin colour and is often found late.</p></div>
  <div class="pn"><a href="#prostate">← Prostate cancer</a><a href="#atlas">Back to the atlas →</a></div>
</section>
<footer>
  <p>Sources: case counts from GLOBOCAN 2022 (IARC/WHO); India rankings from IARC's Global Cancer Observatory India fact sheet (2024 estimates); survival from the US National Cancer Institute's SEER Cancer Stat Facts (diagnosed 2016 to 2022).</p>
  <p>A simplified educational primer, not medical advice. If you notice any of the warning signs here, see a doctor. Most turn out to be something harmless, and the ones that are not are far easier to treat early.</p>

  const views = Array.from(document.querySelectorAll('.view'));
  const ids = views.map(v => v.dataset.view);
  const bar = document.getElementById('barin');
  function show(){
    let h = (location.hash || '').slice(1);
    if (!ids.includes(h)) h = 'atlas';
    views.forEach(v => { v.hidden = v.dataset.view !== h; });
    bar.querySelectorAll('a').forEach(a => {
      const on = a.getAttribute('href') === '#' + h;
      if (on) { a.setAttribute('aria-current', 'page'); try { a.scrollIntoView({block:'nearest', inline:'center'}); } catch(e){} }
      else a.removeAttribute('aria-current');
    });
    const t = h === 'atlas' ? 'The Cancer Atlas' : (document.querySelector('#v-' + h + ' h2').textContent + ' · The Cancer Atlas');
    document.title = t;
  }
  window.addEventListener('hashchange', () => { show(); window.scrollTo(0, 0); });
  show();
})();
</script>
